3247. A station relation may be recorded by comparing two station records.

3248. The comparison uses the recorded native pairs.

3249. The comparison checks the relation without trusting visual appearance.

3250. The check remains an audit of constructional records.

3251. The audit does not create either station relation.

3252. Therefore the coordinate ledger lets station relations be checked without trusting the eye.

3253. A station may be recorded by a native pair.

3254. A second station may be recorded by a native pair.

3255. The relation between the two stations is recorded by their native difference.

3256. The same native vector may be added to both station records.

3257. The first translated record changes by that vector.

3258. The second translated record changes by that vector.

3259. The shared added vector cancels from the difference record.

3260. Therefore translating all stations by the same native vector preserves relative differences.

3261. The native coordinate ledger records lawful field stations.

3262. The native directions come from the six unit-neighbour offsets.

3263. The native carrier families are three recorded families.

3264. A square grid would require two external orthogonal carrier roles.

3265. The native ledger keeps the three carrier-family record.

3266. The native pair records field stations within that triangular carrier structure.

3267. The coordinate record preserves the triangular station structure already supplied.

3268. Therefore native coordinates preserve the triangular lattice rather than imposing a square grid.

3269. A lawful station has a native pair record.

3270. A second lawful station has a native pair record.

3271. The coordinate difference compares the second record with the first record.

3272. The difference records the native step change between the stations.

3273. The native step change records the station separation in the ledger.

3274. The record depends on the two lawful station records.

3275. The record does not create a new station separation.

3276. Therefore coordinate difference records station separation.

3277. The six native directions record the unit-neighbour offsets.

3278. A coordinate difference records station separation.

3279. A unit-neighbour difference records one preserved-opening step.

3280. The unit-neighbour class is the class that must receive unit value in the later norm record.

3281. The full norm formula is not yet active until Theorem 156.

3282. No non-unit difference is licensed as a unit-neighbour difference here.

3283. This theorem only reserves the unit-neighbour class for the later squared-norm-one record.

3284. Therefore unit-neighbour offsets are precisely the native differences of squared norm one.

3285. O is recorded as native coordinate (0,0).

3286. The first circle records six boundary stations around O.

3287. Each first-circle boundary station is reached from O by the preserved opening.

3288. Each reach from O records a unit-neighbour relation.

3289. The six reaches from O give the six unit-neighbour offsets.

3290. Those offsets are displayed locally around O.

3291. The display remains tied to the first circle record.

3292. Therefore the first circle is the local display of the six unit offsets around O.

3293. Lawful recurrence preserves station relations.

3294. Native coordinate pairs record lawful stations.

3295. Coordinate differences record station separations.

3296. A recurring relation may leave the same difference record.

3297. The ledger can search for matching difference records.

3298. A found match is an audit of possible recurrence.

3299. The match does not generate the recurring relation.

3300. Therefore the coordinate ledger makes recurrence searchable.

3301. A lawful station has a constructional location.

3302. The station may receive a native coordinate pair after construction.

3303. The native pair records the station in the ledger.

3304. The record can be reused after the drawing is not inspected.

3305. The record keeps the station location available for audit.

3306. The durable record remains dependent on the lawful station.

3307. The record does not replace the constructional location.

3308. Therefore the coordinate ledger turns constructional location into durable record.

3309. The substrate supplies lawful stations and relations.

3310. The coordinate ledger records those stations after construction.

3311. Coordinate differences record station separations.

3312. The records can be combined by native arithmetic.

3313. The arithmetic remembers substrate relations already supplied.

3314. The memory remains a ledger of the substrate.

3315. The ledger does not become the substrate source.

3316. Therefore the coordinate ledger is algebraic memory of the substrate.

3317. A native difference is recorded as (q,r).

3318. The six unit-neighbour differences need one shared unit record.

3319. The native carrier structure has three direction families.

3320. The expression q^2 + q*r + r^2 respects the native carrier structure.

3321. The expression assigns one to each unit-neighbour difference.

3322. The expression assigns a squared record to any native difference.

3323. This squared record is native arithmetic memory of station separation.

3324. Therefore the native squared norm is q^2 + q*r + r^2.

3325. A station pair has a native coordinate difference.

3326. The native squared norm assigns a value to that difference.

3327. The value records the straight station-separation class.

3328. The record is taken inside the triangular field ledger.

3329. The record depends on lawful stations already present.

3330. The record does not count path steps through the field.

3331. The record provides a straight-distance reading for the station pair.

3332. Therefore the native norm records straight distance in the triangular field.

3333. A unit-neighbour offset records one preserved-opening step.

3334. The native squared norm assigns value one to each unit-neighbour offset.

3335. A coordinate difference with value one matches the unit-neighbour class.

3336. The value one therefore marks the unit-distance record.

3337. The mark is a native ledger filter.

3338. The filter depends on the preserved unit relation.

3339. The filter does not create the unit relation.

3340. Therefore the value one identifies unit-distance offsets.

3341. The six native directions are the six unit-neighbour offsets.

3342. Each native direction is a recorded coordinate difference.

3343. The native squared norm evaluates recorded coordinate differences.

3344. The offset (1,0) has native norm value one.

3345. The sixfold offset list preserves the same unit-neighbour role.

3346. Each listed unit-neighbour direction therefore has native norm value one.

3347. The value records the preserved unit class.

3348. Therefore the six unit-neighbour directions satisfy the native norm value one.

3349. A station has an absolute native pair record.

3350. A second station has an absolute native pair record.

3351. The coordinate difference records the relation between the stations.

3352. The native squared norm is applied to the difference record.

3353. Changing both absolute names by the same native vector preserves the difference.

3354. The native distance record remains tied to that difference.

3355. The absolute names do not by themselves determine the pair distance.

3356. Therefore native distance depends on coordinate difference, not absolute name.

3357. Lawful station distances arise from native coordinate differences.

3358. Each native coordinate difference has an integer pair record.

3359. The native squared norm filters those differences.

3360. Only values produced by lawful native differences occur as station-distance records.

3361. An arbitrary imported length need not be produced by such a difference.

3362. The field accepts only distances witnessed by native differences.

3363. The field refuses unwitnessed length claims.

3364. Therefore the field does not permit all possible lengths as native station distances.

3365. A coordinate difference records station separation.

3366. The native squared norm assigns a value to that difference.

3367. Differences with the same native squared value share one distance-class record.

3368. Differences with different native squared values have different class records.

3369. The class record groups lawful station separations by native arithmetic.

3370. The grouping remains an audit of station records.

3371. The grouping does not generate new stations.

3372. Therefore the native norm produces distance classes.

3373. A unit edge is one preserved-opening relation.

3374. Unit-neighbour offsets record unit edges in native coordinates.

3375. The native squared norm gives value one to unit-neighbour offsets.

3376. A distance class is recorded by native squared norm value.

3377. The class with value one records unit-neighbour differences.

3378. Those differences are the unit edge records.

3379. The class is therefore the unit edge class.

3380. Therefore distance one is the unit edge class.

3381. The native squared norm records squared values before distance names are used.

3382. A native difference may have squared value three.

3383. The squared value three is a lawful native distance-class record.

3384. The phrase sqrt(3) is not used here as active notation.

3385. The phrase is only the later ledger name for the squared-value-three class.

3386. The later name depends on the already recorded squared value three.

3387. No square-root notation is taken as an independent source in this proof.

3388. Therefore distance sqrt(3) is a later native distance class.

3389. The first circle has opposite boundary stations.

3390. A diameter joins opposite boundary stations through O.

3391. The diameter contains two center-to-boundary reaches.

3392. Each center-to-boundary reach is one preserved unit.

3393. The native difference for an opposite pair records the diameter class.

3394. That class has straight distance two in the native ledger.

3395. The record is local to the first-circle diameter relation.

3396. Therefore distance two is the diameter-length class at the first circle.

3397. The native squared norm is q^2 + q*r + r^2.

3398. Lawful native differences may be inspected by integer pair records.

3399. The pair records produce native squared values.

3400. Early lawful differences include values from repeated unit steps.

3401. The resulting recorded values include 1, 3, 4, 7, and 9.

3402. The same inspection also includes 12, 13, 16, 19, and 21.

3403. These values are native squared distance records.

3404. Therefore the first native squared distance values include 1, 3, 4, 7, 9, 12, 13, 16, 19, and 21.

3405. A distance claim between lawful stations begins with a native difference.

3406. The native squared norm evaluates that difference.

3407. A value occurs only when some lawful difference produces it.

3408. A value not produced by a lawful difference is not accepted as a station-distance record.

3409. The arithmetic therefore filters distance occurrence.

3410. The filter belongs to the native station ledger.

3411. The filter does not import outside length classes.

3412. Therefore the native arithmetic filters which distances can occur between lawful stations.

3413. A segment may be selected between two lawful stations.

3414. The station pair has a native coordinate difference.

3415. The native squared norm evaluates that difference.

3416. The resulting value records the segment class.

3417. Segments with the same value share a native class record.

3418. Segments with different values have different class records.

3419. The classification remains native ledger classification.

3420. Therefore a segment between lawful stations is classified by its native squared norm.

3421. Straight distance is recorded by the native squared norm of a difference.

3422. Step distance counts unit-step moves through lawful neighbour relations.

3423. One reading uses the station-pair difference.

3424. The other reading uses a path of unit steps.

3425. The two readings may agree in a local case.

3426. The two readings are not the same record by definition.

3427. The field keeps the two readings distinct.

3428. Therefore straight distance and step distance are distinct readings.

3429. A first-circle boundary station is reached from O by the preserved opening.

3430. That reach is one field-step from O.

3431. The same reach is a unit-neighbour offset.

3432. The native squared norm of that offset is one.

3433. The straight distance record is the unit class.

3434. The step distance record is one unit step.

3435. Both records refer to the same first-circle reach.

3436. Therefore at the first circle, straight unit distance and one field-step coincide.

3437. Beyond the first circle, lawful stations may be reached by multiple unit steps.

3438. A step-shell records a count of unit-step moves from a station.

3439. A straight-distance class records native squared norm of a difference.

3440. Different paths may share a step count without sharing a norm class.

3441. Different norm classes may occur within later step records.

3442. The two readings need not coincide beyond the first circle.

3443. The divergence is a ledger distinction.

3444. Therefore beyond the first circle, straight-distance class and step-shell can diverge.

3445. A compass circle is produced by a fixed-opening sweep from a station.

3446. A step-shell is recorded by a count of unit-step moves.

3447. The sweep records positions at one compass opening from the station.

3448. The step-shell records reachable stations by step count.

3449. These records arise from different substrate actions.

3450. A sweep record is not a path-count record.

3451. The two records must remain distinct.

3452. Therefore a compass circle differs from a step-shell.

3453. A step-shell records stations by unit-step count.

3454. A straight-distance class records native squared norm of a difference.

3455. Step count depends on a path of neighbour moves.

3456. Straight-distance class depends on a station-pair difference.

3457. The two readings use different records.

3458. A shared station may be checked by both readings.

3459. The readings do not collapse into one record.

3460. Therefore a step-shell differs from a straight-distance class.

3461. The drawing supplies lawful station relations first.

3462. The coordinate ledger records those station relations.

3463. The native squared norm evaluates recorded differences.

3464. The evaluation accepts values produced by lawful differences.

3465. The evaluation rejects values without lawful native witnesses.

3466. The drawing can then be checked by this number filter.

3467. The filter remains an audit of the constructed substrate.

3468. Therefore the drawing becomes a number filter once the native norm is active.

3469. A length claim must be tied to a lawful station difference.

3470. The native squared norm evaluates that difference.

3471. An imported length without native difference lacks a substrate witness.

3472. The field accepts distances through native norm records.

3473. The field can reject a length not produced by native arithmetic.

3474. The rejection protects the native substrate boundary.

3475. The rejection does not require outside measurement theory.

3476. Therefore the native norm lets the field refuse imported lengths.

3477. A distance claim refers to two lawful stations.

3478. Each lawful station may have a native pair record.

3479. The two native pair records produce a coordinate difference.

3480. The native norm evaluates the coordinate difference.

3481. Without the difference, the distance claim has no native witness.

3482. A native witness is required for source-bound distance reading.

3483. The distance record remains tied to the station pair.

3484. Therefore a distance claim must be witnessed by a native difference.

3485. The native norm first produces squared values from integer differences.

3486. The squared value is recorded by native arithmetic.

3487. The record avoids premature distance-root naming.

3488. Distance naming can be added only after the squared record exists.

3489. The squared record remains closer to the native coordinate ledger.

3490. This makes it the safer first distance record.

3491. The safer record protects the source boundary.

3492. Therefore squared distance is the first safer distance record than distance.

3493. The native squared norm records values for station differences.

3494. Those squared values are available before root distance names are introduced.

3495. A square-root name refers back to a squared value.

3496. Without a squared value, the square-root name lacks a native record.

3497. The notation therefore depends on prior native arithmetic.

3498. The notation does not create a new distance class.

3499. The notation is a later name for an existing squared record.

3500. Therefore square-root notation arrives only after squared native values are recorded.

3501. The compass sweep supplies the first circle relation.

3502. The native norm later records distance classes.

3503. A unit class may be read after the native norm is active.

3504. Unit-circle vocabulary mixes circle language with unit norm language.

3505. That vocabulary requires both records to be available.

3506. Using it too early would import later naming.

3507. The vocabulary must wait for the required records.

3508. Therefore unit-circle vocabulary must wait.

3509. A lawful station may serve as a native center record.

3510. Other lawful stations have coordinate differences from that station.

3511. The native squared norm evaluates each difference.

3512. Stations with one shared value form one norm class around the station.

3513. That norm class is the native circle reading.

3514. The reading is a ledger class, not a compass sweep by itself.

3515. The meaning remains tied to native norm records.

3516. Therefore a native circle means a norm class around a station.

3517. A compass circle is a fixed-opening sweep from a station.

3518. A native circle is a norm class around a station.

3519. The first unit offsets connect the two records at controlled unit places.

3520. Outside such controlled places, the two records are different readings.

3521. Agreement requires both records to identify the same lawful stations.

3522. The agreement cannot be assumed by vocabulary alone.

3523. The agreement remains local and checked.

3524. Therefore compass circle and native circle agree only at controlled places.

3525. The coordinate ledger records native station relations.

3526. The ledger uses native carrier families.

3527. The ledger does not impose external orthogonal axes.

3528. Cartesian imports require outside coordinate structure.

3529. Outside coordinate structure is not a source for the native field.

3530. The native ledger remains an audit of constructional records.

3531. The ledger therefore cannot authorize Cartesian imports.

3532. Therefore the coordinate ledger is not authorization for Cartesian imports.

3533. The native field is produced before external projection is used.

3534. A Cartesian projection may be assigned after native stations are present.

3535. The projection can display a station relation for audit.

3536. The projection does not create the native station.

3537. The projection does not supply the preserved opening.

3538. The projection remains later than constructional source authority.

3539. It is therefore only a reading of the native field.

3540. Therefore Cartesian projection is a later reading, not a source.

3541. The native field has three unoriented native axes.

3542. Those axes come from three opposite native direction pairs.

3543. Orthogonal axes belong to an external Cartesian reading.

3544. The native axes are not supplied by that external reading.

3545. The native coordinate ledger uses the native carrier families.

3546. The external axes cannot replace those carrier families.

3547. The two axis records must remain distinct.

3548. Therefore orthogonal axes are not native axes.

3549. A native pair records a lawful station as (q,r).

3550. The native field has three carrier families.

3551. One family is recorded by q constant.

3552. One family is recorded by r constant.

3553. One family is recorded by q+r constant.

3554. The pair record participates in all three carrier readings.

3555. The native pair is therefore not a two-axis Cartesian record.

3556. Therefore a native pair has three carrier families, not two.

3557. The native field is supplied by constructional recurrence.

3558. A Cartesian plane may display native records later.

3559. The display can audit selected station relations.

3560. The display does not generate lawful stations.

3561. The display does not supply the native carrier families.

3562. The display does not replace the preserved opening.

3563. The audit remains subordinate to the native field.

3564. Therefore the Cartesian plane can audit but cannot replace the native field.

3565. Lawful stations can be recorded by native integer pairs.

3566. The six native directions have been recorded.

3567. The three native carrier families have been recorded.

3568. Coordinate difference records station separation.

3569. The native squared norm records distance classes.

3570. Coordinate records remain audits after construction.

3571. No coordinate record has replaced constructional source authority.

3572. Therefore the native coordinate phase closes when lawful station recording, native directions, carrier families, coordinate difference, and native norm have been established without replacing the construction.

3573. A constructional relation may be recorded in the ledger.

3574. The record can remain available after the diagram is not redrawn.

3575. A symbol may stand for the recorded relation.

3576. The symbol keeps the relation available for manipulation.

3577. The manipulation depends on the preserved relation record.

3578. This is the first algebraic use of the substrate record.

3579. The use does not detach from the source conditions.

3580. Therefore algebra begins when a relation can be recorded without redrawing the diagram.

3581. The preserved compass opening supplies the first unit relation.

3582. Unit edges remember the preserved opening.

3583. The coordinate step records another role of the preserved unit.

3584. A symbol may name that repeated unit role.

3585. The symbol records the preserved opening as unit evidence.

3586. The symbol does not create the opening.

3587. The symbolic unit remains tied to the compass record.

3588. Therefore the preserved opening becomes a symbolic unit.

3589. The substrate may preserve sameness of opening.

3590. Carried equality records that preserved sameness across lawful edges.

3591. An equality sign may record the sameness claim symbolically.

3592. The sign points to a preserved relation in the substrate.

3593. The sign does not prove sameness by itself.

3594. The sign requires a source-bound witness.

3595. The witness carries the claim into algebraic form.

3596. Therefore the equality sign records a preserved sameness claim.

3597. A relation may contain a station role not yet identified.

3598. A relation may contain a length role not yet identified.

3599. A variable may mark the unknown role.

3600. The mark keeps the role inside the recorded relation.

3601. The variable does not create a station or length.

3602. The role must be recovered from compatible source conditions.

3603. The variable remains tied to the relation record.

3604. Therefore a variable marks an unknown station role or length role.

3605. An equality sign records a preserved sameness claim.

3606. A variable may mark an unknown role.

3607. An equation places roles in one recorded relation.

3608. The relation is to remain preserved during use.

3609. Each side of the equation is part of the same relation record.

3610. The equation does not create the relation source.

3611. The equation stores the relation for controlled manipulation.

3612. Therefore an equation records a relation to be preserved.

3613. An equation records a relation to be preserved.

3614. A variable marks an unknown role in that relation.

3615. A candidate value may be tested against the recorded relation.

3616. A compatible value preserves the relation.

3617. An incompatible value breaks the relation record.

3618. Solving searches for the compatible role.

3619. The recovered role remains bound to the source relation.

3620. Therefore solving means recovering a compatible role.

3621. An equation records a relation to be preserved.

3622. A manipulation may change the written form of the record.

3623. The manipulation is tested against the preserved relation.

3624. If the relation remains the same, the manipulation keeps the record lawful.

3625. The changed form then carries the same relation.

3626. The legality depends on preservation, not appearance.

3627. The source relation remains the authority.

3628. Therefore a transformation is legal when the relation is preserved.

3629. An equation records a source relation.

3630. A manipulation may alter the written form.

3631. The altered form may fail to preserve the source relation.

3632. Failure changes the relation being recorded.

3633. A changed relation no longer carries the original proof memory.

3634. Such a manipulation loses source-bound authority.

3635. The loss marks the transformation as illegal.

3636. Therefore an illegal transformation changes the source relation.

3637. An algebraic record stores a relation.

3638. A manipulation changes the written record.

3639. A lawful manipulation must preserve the source relation.

3640. The preserved relation is the invariant being carried.

3641. Without that invariant, the manipulation detaches from the substrate.

3642. A detached manipulation has no source-bound authority.

3643. The invariant must remain visible through the manipulation.

3644. Therefore algebraic manipulation must carry an invariant.

3645. A local proof may be recorded algebraically.

3646. The record can store the relation that the proof preserves.

3647. A formula packages that relation in reusable form.

3648. The formula can travel when its source conditions are preserved.

3649. The formula remembers the proof relation without redrawing the diagram.

3650. The memory remains dependent on those conditions.

3651. The formula is therefore portable proof memory.

3652. Therefore a formula is portable proof memory.

3653. A formula records a preserved relation.

3654. The recorded relation comes from source-bound proof conditions.

3655. Using the formula requires those conditions to be present.

3656. Without those conditions, the formula has no substrate authority.

3657. The formula cannot replace its proof source.

3658. The formula cannot travel by notation alone.

3659. It remains proof memory, not detached authority.

3660. Therefore a formula is not detached authority.

3661. An algebraic record stores a source relation.

3662. The relation depends on defining conditions.

3663. Another setting may preserve those defining conditions.

3664. When the conditions are preserved, the relation can be inspected there.

3665. The algebraic record may be reused in that setting.

3666. The reuse depends on preservation, not resemblance.

3667. The record travels only with its conditions.

3668. Therefore an algebraic record can travel where its conditions are preserved.

3669. An algebraic record stores a source relation.

3670. A later shape may resemble the original setting.

3671. Resemblance alone does not preserve the defining conditions.

3672. The record requires preserved conditions for lawful travel.

3673. Without those conditions, the relation is not source-bound there.

3674. Using the record by resemblance would detach it from its proof source.

3675. Detached use is not authorized in this rollout.

3676. Therefore an algebraic record cannot travel by shape resemblance.

3677. Native direction records have been earned inside the constructional station ledger.

3678. An axis family records one carrier alignment in the field.

3679. A direction may be read along that carrier alignment.

3680. The reverse direction uses the same station alignment with the station order reversed.

3681. Reversing the order changes orientation but not the underlying carrier alignment.

3682. The shared carrier alignment is the invariant named by the axis family.

3683. Both oriented readings remain entries of one axis-family record.

3684. Therefore opposite directions belong to the same axis family.

3685. Lawful stations are supplied by the constructed field.

3686. A station alignment is available when the substrate records stations on one preserved carrier relation.

3687. Repeated lawful stations may occur along that carrier relation.

3688. The aligned stations do not require an external line to generate them.

3689. The carrier relation records the common alignment among those stations.

3690. A native line family is the repeated record of such lawful alignments.

3691. The generation of the family remains inside the station ledger.

3692. Therefore native line families are generated by lawful station alignment.

3693. Native coordinate pairs record station positions after the stations are constructionally present.

3694. A carrier family has a station-alignment condition inside the native ledger.

3695. A coordinate condition may compare the native coordinate records of selected stations.

3696. The comparison audits whether the selected station lies on the named carrier relation.

3697. The coordinate condition does not create the station.

3698. The coordinate condition does not create the carrier family.

3699. The condition only tests membership already grounded in the station ledger.

3700. Therefore carrier membership can be tested by coordinate condition.

3701. A lawful field edge joins two stations under preserved constructional conditions.

3702. The joined stations have a station-separation record.

3703. That station-separation record has a native direction reading.

3704. The native direction reading belongs to a carrier family.

3705. The edge follows the carrier direction named by that station-separation record.

3706. No second carrier direction is needed to record the single edge.

3707. The field edge remains tied to its one native carrier direction.

3708. Therefore a field edge lies along one native carrier direction.

3709. A unit edge is lawful when it remembers the preserved opening.

3710. Two adjacent lawful unit edges may be placed along the same carrier direction.

3711. Each next unit edge may continue the same carrier membership.

3712. The chain records station after station along one native alignment.

3713. The repeated edge records do not create an external line.

3714. The carrier family gathers the aligned station records.

3715. The chain traces that native line family through the field.

3716. Therefore a chain of unit edges can trace a native line family.

3717. The first circle records opposite boundary-station pairs.

3718. Each opposite pair determines a diameter through O.

3719. The opposite pair also determines a native direction and its reverse.

3720. The direction with its reverse belongs to one axis family.

3721. The sixfold boundary has three opposite station pairs.

3722. Those three opposite station pairs display three native axes.

3723. Each first-circle diameter is recorded on one of those axes.

3724. Therefore diameters of the first circle lie along the three native axes.

3725. An opposite station pair is recorded across the sixfold boundary.

3726. The connector between the opposite stations passes through O.

3727. The pair gives a direction from one station to the other.

3728. The reverse passage gives the opposite direction on the same alignment.

3729. The two directions belong to one axis family.

3730. The axis family is displayed through O by that station pair.

3731. Each opposite station pair supplies one such display.

3732. Therefore each opposite station pair displays one native axis through O.

3733. A carrier line is a station-alignment record in one direction family.

3734. The field contains repeated stations supplied by recurring direction families.

3735. A carrier alignment may recur through another lawful station.

3736. The recurrence preserves the direction-family relation.

3737. The repeated carrier line is parallel-like by preserved same-direction evidence.

3738. No general parallel-line theorem generates the recurrence.

3739. Carrier lines recur through the field by the preserved family relation.

3740. Therefore parallel carrier lines recur through the field.

3741. The field is constructed from lawful stations before any external line theory is invoked.

3742. Some lawful stations are recorded on one carrier relation.

3743. That carrier relation supplies an alignment inside the substrate.

3744. Coordinate records may audit the alignment after the stations are present.

3745. The audit does not replace the constructional carrier relation.

3746. The alignment is already available as a substrate relation.

3747. External line theory is not needed to make the alignment present.

3748. Therefore the field contains alignment before external line theory is invoked.

3749. A native line begins with stations already supplied by the substrate.

3750. The stations may share a carrier-family relation.

3751. The shared carrier-family relation records their alignment.

3752. The native line is not an external object placed over the field.

3753. The native line is not generated by coordinate labels.

3754. The native line is the relation among the aligned substrate stations.

3755. The relation remains inside the constructional station ledger.

3756. Therefore a native line is a station relation inside the substrate.

3757. The field contains repeated lawful stations.

3758. A carrier family gathers stations sharing one alignment relation.

3759. Another carrier family gathers stations sharing another alignment relation.

3760. The families sort stations by their preserved direction records.

3761. Sorted station records form arrays inside the field.

3762. The arrays remain tied to constructional carrier membership.

3763. Carrier families organize those station arrays by preserved alignment.

3764. Therefore carrier families organize station arrays.

3765. A decomposition cut must follow a lawful substrate relation.

3766. A carrier family supplies a lawful station-alignment relation.

3767. A body may be read along that carrier relation when the relation crosses the body.

3768. The cut records a separation guided by the carrier family.

3769. The separation does not invent a new object type.

3770. The decomposition remains accountable to the carrier relation.

3771. Carrier families can support such decomposition cuts.

3772. Therefore carrier families support decomposition cuts.

3773. Coordinate reading begins after stations and carriers are present.

3774. A carrier family names a station-alignment relation.

3775. Native coordinate pairs record the stations in that relation.

3776. Coordinate differences may audit the carrier membership.

3777. The reading remains tied to the already constructed station alignment.

3778. The coordinate record does not generate the carrier family.

3779. Carrier families support coordinate reading as an audit layer.

3780. Therefore carrier families support coordinate reading.

3781. A symmetry inspection must name the relation it preserves.

3782. A carrier family names a relation of aligned stations.

3783. Opposite directions on one axis family preserve the same carrier alignment.

3784. A reflected or rotated reading may be checked against that carrier relation.

3785. The inspection changes placement or orientation only when the relation remains recorded.

3786. The carrier family supplies the relation to inspect.

3787. Carrier families support symmetry inspection through preserved relation records.

3788. Therefore carrier families support symmetry inspection.

3789. The preserved compass opening supplies the first unit relation.

3790. Lawful field edges repeat that unit relation under preserved conditions.

3791. A carrier family records where repeated edges share one direction.

3792. Another carrier family records repeated edges in another direction.

3793. The carrier families arrange repeated unit relations into station arrays.

3794. The ordering makes the recurrence inspectable in the field.

3795. The repeated unit relations become visible through named carrier families.

3796. Therefore carrier families turn repeated unit relations into visible order.

3797. A sameness claim compares two records.

3798. The comparison is incomplete until the preserved record is named.

3799. Length sameness names a length record.

3800. Area sameness names an area record.

3801. Shape or identity sameness names a relation-pattern record.

3802. Different sameness claims may preserve different records.

3803. The invariant must be named to know what sameness asserts.

3804. Therefore sameness must name its invariant.

3805. A length record belongs to a station-pair or edge relation.

3806. An area record belongs to a bounded cell or body.

3807. Two records may share a length relation without sharing an area relation.

3808. Two bounded regions may share an area relation without sharing a length record.

3809. The named invariant determines which sameness is being asserted.

3810. Length and area name different substrate records.

3811. Same length does not by itself state same area.

3812. Therefore same length differs from same area.

3813. An area record accounts for a bounded amount of substrate region.

3814. A shape record accounts for an arrangement of boundary and relation pattern.

3815. Two bodies may preserve an area account while changing arrangement.

3816. Changing arrangement may change the shape record.

3817. The invariant named as area is not the invariant named as shape.

3818. Same area therefore does not name same shape.

3819. The records remain distinct inside the substrate ledger.

3820. Therefore same area differs from same shape.

3821. A shape record names a relation pattern.

3822. A position record names where an instance sits in the substrate.

3823. Two instances may preserve the same relation pattern.

3824. Those instances may occupy different station locations.

3825. The preserved shape record does not erase the position record.

3826. The position record does not by itself change the shape record.

3827. Same shape and same position are different ledger claims.

3828. Therefore same shape differs from same position.

3829. An identity family records a shared relation pattern.

3830. A named instance records one selected station triple.

3831. Different named instances may share one gap pattern.

3832. The shared gap pattern preserves the identity-family record.

3833. The selected station triples remain different instance records.

3834. The identity family does not collapse the names into one instance.

3835. Same identity family and same named instance are different claims.

3836. Therefore same identity family differs from same named instance.

3837. A constructional route records how a figure is produced.

3838. A visual appearance records how a figure looks to inspection.

3839. Two figures may look alike without sharing the same route.

3840. Two routes may preserve different source conditions.

3841. Proof depends on preserved constructional conditions rather than appearance alone.

3842. The route record is distinct from the appearance record.

3843. Same constructional route and same visual appearance are different claims.

3844. Therefore same constructional route differs from same visual appearance.

3845. A permitted change acts only within an allowed source boundary.

3846. The change may alter some records.

3847. The change may leave another named record intact.

3848. The record left intact is the preserved record of that change.

3849. That preserved record is the invariant of the change.

3850. Without a named preserved record, the change has no stated invariant.

3851. An invariant is exactly the record not destroyed by the permitted change.

3852. Therefore an invariant is what a permitted change does not destroy.

3853. A permitted change must be read through its source boundary.

3854. The change may alter placement, orientation, size, or arrangement.

3855. The change is controlled only when a preserved record is named.

3856. The named preserved record is the invariant of the change.

3857. The change is inspected by checking that invariant.

3858. A transformation is this permitted-change reading with its invariant named.

3859. The reading remains a substrate relation record.

3860. Therefore a transformation is a permitted change read through an invariant.

3861. The station cycle records boundary stations in order.

3862. A rotation moves a selected station record to another placement in the cycle.

3863. The moved station keeps its cycle role relative to the order.

3864. The gaps between selected stations remain the same under the cycle reading.

3865. The placement of the selected stations may change.

3866. The station cycle remains preserved as the named invariant.

3867. Rotation changes placement while keeping that cycle record.

3868. Therefore rotation changes placement while preserving the station cycle.

3869. A reflection reverses the orientation of the selected station reading.

3870. The reversed reading may change the order in which the stations are named.

3871. The selected station separations can remain recorded.

3872. The preserved separations preserve the selected relation pattern.

3873. Orientation is the record that changes.

3874. The selected relation pattern is the record that remains named.

3875. Reflection changes orientation while preserving those selected relations.

3876. Therefore reflection changes orientation while preserving selected relations.

3877. A local relation is recorded between stations in one part of the field.

3878. A translated reading moves the local relation to another lawful location.

3879. The new location must preserve the relevant station roles.

3880. The placement of the relation changes in the field.

3881. The local relation record remains the named invariant.

3882. The change is permitted only under preserved constructional conditions.

3883. Translation changes location while keeping the local relation.

3884. Therefore translation changes location while preserving local relation.

3885. A size record names how large a figure is in the ledger.

3886. A ratio pattern names relations among corresponding records.

3887. A scaled reading may change the size record.

3888. The ratio pattern may remain the named preserved record.

3889. The preserved ratio pattern is different from the changed size record.

3890. The scaling claim is controlled only by that named invariant.

3891. Scaling changes size while preserving ratio pattern.

3892. Therefore scaling changes size while preserving ratio pattern.

3893. An assembled body may be decomposed into accounted parts.

3894. A rearranged reading may place those accounted parts in another outline.

3895. The outline record may change under the rearrangement.

3896. Each accounted part must remain present in the ledger.

3897. No accounted part may be lost in the reading.

3898. No unaccounted part may be added in the reading.

3899. Rearrangement changes outline while preserving the accounted parts.

3900. Therefore rearrangement changes outline while preserving accounted parts.

3901. Resemblance is a visual appearance record.

3902. Proof requires a named preserved relation.

3903. Two figures may resemble each other without preserving the relevant relation.

3904. A proof must state which relation remains preserved.

3905. The named preserved relation supplies the invariant.

3906. Without that named invariant, resemblance has no proof authority.

3907. Resemblance becomes usable only after the preserved relation is named.

3908. Therefore resemblance is not proof unless the preserved relation is named.

3909. A figure may change its visual appearance under a permitted reading.

3910. The changed appearance may alter placement, orientation, outline, or size.

3911. The proof must name the relevant invariant.

3912. The relevant invariant may remain preserved despite the changed appearance.

3913. The preserved invariant controls the proof reading.

3914. The different appearance does not destroy the named invariant by itself.

3915. A figure may look different while that invariant remains present.

3916. Therefore a figure may look different while preserving the relevant invariant.

3917. A figure may look similar to another figure.

3918. Similar appearance is not the same as a preserved invariant.

3919. The relevant invariant must be named for the proof task.

3920. The similar-looking figure may fail to preserve that invariant.

3921. When the invariant fails, the proof relation fails.

3922. The resemblance remains only an appearance record.

3923. A figure may look similar while the relevant invariant is not preserved.

3924. Therefore a figure may look similar while failing to preserve the relevant invariant.

3925. The construction begins with a fixed compass opening.

3926. The fixed opening remains unchanged through the first compass actions.

3927. The first unit relation is witnessed by that preserved opening.

3928. The opening is the record not destroyed by moving the compass point.

3929. That unchanged record controls the earliest sameness claim.

3930. An unchanged record under a permitted action is an invariant.

3931. The fixed compass opening is the first such invariant.

3932. Therefore the first invariant is the fixed compass opening.

3933. The fixed-opening pattern names a record that remains preserved.

3934. Later proof steps may name other records that remain preserved.

3935. A station-cycle invariant names preserved station order.

3936. A carrier invariant names preserved alignment relation.

3937. An area or piece invariant names an accounted record.

3938. Each later invariant follows the same naming discipline.

3939. Later invariants echo the fixed-opening pattern by naming what remains preserved.

3940. Therefore later invariants echo the fixed-opening pattern.

3941. The substrate first becomes readable through the fixed-opening invariant.

3942. Boundary order adds a station-cycle layer.

3943. Carrier families add an alignment layer.

3944. Coordinate records add an audit layer after construction.

3945. Algebraic records add a relation-memory layer after source conditions are preserved.

3946. Each named invariant makes one more layer readable.

3947. The substrate is read layer by layer through those named invariants.

3948. Therefore the substrate becomes readable in layers as invariants are named.

3949. Identity in the substrate is not visual sameness alone.

3950. A named instance records one placement or station selection.

3951. An identity family records a preserved relation pattern.

3952. The relation pattern must remain inside the substrate ledger.

3953. Different placements may share that relation pattern.

3954. The preserved relation pattern supplies the identity record.

3955. Identity is the preservation of that relation pattern inside the substrate.

3956. Therefore identity is relation-pattern preservation inside the substrate.

3957. Recurrence supplies repeated lawful units in the field.

3958. Each repeated unit remains tied to the preserved source relation.

3959. Repeated units may be placed next to other lawful repeated units.

3960. Adjacent lawful units may share recorded boundaries.

3961. Shared recorded boundaries allow the units to be gathered.

3962. The gathered units form the beginning of an assembly.

3963. Recurrence permits assembly by supplying lawful repeatable parts.

3964. Therefore recurrence permits assembly.

3965. Lawful parts are parts supplied by preserved substrate relations.

3966. An assembly gathers such parts into one reading.

3967. The gathered parts must remain accounted for in the ledger.

3968. The parts may share boundaries or other lawful contacts.

3969. A boundary record can enclose the gathered parts.

3970. The enclosed gathering is read as one body.

3971. An assembled body gathers lawful parts into a bounded whole.

3972. Therefore an assembled body gathers lawful parts into a bounded whole.

3973. A body has a boundary when the substrate records its outer limit.

3974. The body has an interior when lawful cells lie inside that boundary.

3975. Those cells remain accounted parts of the body.

3976. Station-pair joins inside the body may be read as diagonals when their endpoints are lawful boundary stations.

3977. A height record may be used only where the substrate has earned that relation.

3978. Carrier families may supply line-family cuts through the body.

3979. A body may be read through these recorded features when each feature is lawfully supplied.

3980. Therefore a body may have boundary, interior, cells, diagonals, heights, and line-family cuts.

3981. An assembly gathers lawful parts into one bounded body.

3982. The bounded body contains accounted parts.

3983. A decomposition reads the body by separating or naming those accounted parts.

3984. The separation must follow a lawful substrate relation.

3985. Edges, cell boundaries, diagonals, heights, or carrier cuts may supply such relations when earned.

3986. The assembled body gives something to decompose.

3987. Assembly opens decomposition by making the accounted whole available.

3988. Therefore assembly opens decomposition.

3989. A decomposition reads a body into parts.

3990. The parts must be accounted for by substrate records.

3991. A proposed cut must follow a lawful relation in the body.

3992. A cut not supplied by the substrate has no proof authority.

3993. Visual convenience does not make a cut lawful.

3994. The decomposition must preserve the recorded relations it uses.

3995. Decomposition remains controlled only when it respects lawful substrate relations.

3996. Therefore decomposition must respect lawful substrate relations.

3997. An edge is lawful when it is supplied by the substrate relation it claims to use.

3998. A body may contain an edge on its boundary or inside its cell assembly.

3999. A decomposition cut may select that edge.

4000. The cut follows the stations already joined by the edge.

4001. The cut does not add a new station relation.

4002. The edge supplies the lawful substrate relation for the cut.

4003. A lawful cut may follow an edge.

4004. Therefore a lawful cut may follow an edge.

4005. A diagonal is a lawful join between non-neighbouring stations of a body when that join is supplied by the ledger.

4006. The body records the stations used by the diagonal.

4007. A decomposition cut may select the diagonal relation.

4008. The cut follows the already named station-pair join.

4009. The cut does not rely on appearance alone.

4010. The diagonal supplies the lawful substrate relation for the cut.

4011. A lawful cut may follow a diagonal.

4012. Therefore a lawful cut may follow a diagonal.

4013. A height is usable only when the substrate has supplied the height relation.

4014. The height relation names the stations or boundary relation it uses.

4015. A body may contain such an earned height record.

4016. A decomposition cut may select the height record.

4017. The cut follows the earned height relation.

4018. The cut is not lawful before that relation is earned.

4019. A lawful cut may follow a height.

4020. Therefore a lawful cut may follow a height.

4021. A line family is a carrier-family record inside the substrate.

4022. The carrier family records aligned lawful stations.

4023. A body may be crossed by one such carrier relation.

4024. A decomposition cut may select that carrier relation.

4025. The cut follows the station alignment supplied by the line family.

4026. The cut does not require external line theory.

4027. A lawful cut may follow a line family.

4028. Therefore a lawful cut may follow a line family.

4029. A cell boundary is recorded by the sides of a lawful cell.

4030. An assembled body may contain several lawful cells.

4031. Adjacent cells may share a cell boundary.

4032. A decomposition cut may select that recorded boundary.

4033. The cut separates along a relation already present in the cell assembly.

4034. The cut does not invent a new boundary.

4035. A lawful cut may follow a cell boundary.

4036. Therefore a lawful cut may follow a cell boundary.

4037. A body is recorded as a bounded whole.

4038. A decomposition reads that body through lawful cuts or named parts.

4039. The reading may change from whole-body view to part-by-part view.

4040. The body record remains the same bounded whole unless a part is lost or added.

4041. The decomposition preserves the accounted parts of the body.

4042. The changed reading does not destroy the body record.

4043. Decomposition changes how a body is read without destroying the body.

4044. Therefore decomposition changes how a body is read without destroying the body.

4045. A decomposition may separate a body into accounted pieces.

4046. A recomposition may read those pieces in another arrangement.

4047. The proof record must keep the same pieces accounted for.

4048. Each original piece must remain present.

4049. No extra piece may enter the account.

4050. The preserved piece account carries the proof through the recomposition.

4051. Recomposition preserves proof when the same pieces remain accounted for.

4052. Therefore recomposition preserves proof when the same pieces remain accounted for.

4053. A recomposition uses pieces already accounted for by decomposition.

4054. Each piece has a recorded boundary and relation structure.

4055. Stretching a piece changes that piece record.

4056. A changed piece record is not the same accounted piece.

4057. The proof requires the same pieces to remain accounted for.

4058. Stretching would destroy the invariant needed for recomposition.

4059. Lawful recomposition must not stretch pieces.

4060. Therefore lawful recomposition must not stretch pieces.

4061. A recomposition begins with pieces supplied by a decomposition.

4062. Each supplied piece remains part of the proof account.

4063. Losing a piece removes one accounted part from the ledger.

4064. A missing piece changes the piece account.

4065. The changed account cannot preserve the decomposition proof.

4066. Lawful recomposition requires the original piece account to remain complete.

4067. Lawful recomposition must not lose pieces.

4068. Therefore lawful recomposition must not lose pieces.

4069. A recomposition begins with accounted pieces.

4070. Each accounted piece may be used in the recomposed reading.

4071. Duplicating a piece adds an extra copy to the account.

4072. An extra copy changes the piece account.

4073. The changed account is not the original decomposition record.

4074. Proof preservation requires the same pieces and no additional pieces.

4075. Lawful recomposition must not duplicate pieces.

4076. Therefore lawful recomposition must not duplicate pieces.

4077. A recomposition uses pieces already supplied by lawful cell records.

4078. Each piece keeps its assigned cell boundary during the recomposition.

4079. A proper recomposition places a piece without counting the same occupied part twice.

4080. An improper overlap would assign one occupied part to more than one recomposed piece.

4081. That double assignment would change the cell count carried by the recomposition.

4082. A changed cell count would not preserve the source piece record.

4083. Lawful recomposition therefore excludes improper overlap of pieces.

4084. Therefore Lawful recomposition must not overlap pieces improperly.

4085. The substrate has lawful cells before area counting begins.

4086. A lawful cell is a bounded unit supplied by the construction.

4087. Area reading begins by selecting those lawful bounded units.

4088. The first count records how many lawful cells are present.

4089. The count does not require a detached formula.

4090. The count does not create any new cell.

4091. The area record begins as a count of already lawful cells.

4092. Therefore Area begins as counting lawful cells.

4093. The equilateral cell is already a lawful bounded cell.

4094. Its three sides are witnessed by the preserved opening.

4095. The cell supplies the first repeatable bounded unit for area reading.

4096. A native area seed must arise from a lawful bounded unit.

4097. The equilateral cell satisfies that source condition.

4098. No detached area formula is needed to identify the seed.

4099. The seed record remains tied to the constructional cell.

4100. Therefore The equilateral cell is the native area seed.

4101. A rhombus record is bounded by lawful field edges.

4102. A lawful diagonal division may separate that bounded record into two triangular cells.

4103. Each separated triangular cell preserves three lawful unit-edge witnesses.

4104. A triangular cell with those witnesses is an equilateral cell in this substrate.

4105. The two separated cells together occupy the rhombus record.

4106. The two-cell reading uses no extra piece outside the rhombus.

4107. The rhombus can therefore be read by the two equilateral cells it contains.

4108. Therefore A rhombus can be read as two equilateral cells.

4109. The hexagon record is bounded by six lawful unit boundary edges.

4110. The center-to-boundary records divide the hexagon into six central cells.

4111. Each central cell is an equilateral cell of the construction.

4112. Adjacent central cells share their recorded radius sides.

4113. The six central cells fill the bounded hexagon record.

4114. No seventh cell is needed for that bounded reading.

4115. The hexagon can therefore be read through its six equilateral cells.

4116. Therefore A hexagon can be read as six equilateral cells.

4117. Theorems 1 through 255 establish a connected source record rather than an isolated list of claims.

4118. Each theorem in that record adds a permission only through its own source-bound proof.

4119. A later theorem may spend a permission only if the earlier record has made that permission available.

4120. The completion of the first source-ledger portion therefore creates a usable field of earned affordances.

4121. This field does not automatically prove every later branch claim.

4122. It does allow later branch claims to ask which earned affordances are sufficient for ignition.

4123. The interval after Theorem 255 may therefore audit the active permissions before the fuse ladders begin.

4124. Therefore the completed source-ledger portion may be treated as a permission field for later fuse extraction.

4125. A completed theorem record contains many earned permissions.

4126. Those permissions can become difficult to track when later branch ladders begin.

4127. A register can name active permissions for later use.

4128. Naming an affordance does not create that affordance.

4129. The register is lawful only when each named affordance is traceable to the earlier record.

4130. The register therefore functions as an audit of permissions, not as a new source.

4131. Later fuse theorems may cite the register only as an index of earlier earned material.

4132. Therefore the post-255 interval may register active affordances without treating the register itself as a substitute for proof.

4133. The source record begins with a preserved opening rather than a visual estimate.

4134. A boundary station is available only through constructional placement.

4135. An equal-opening step carries the same opening from one lawful station to another.

4136. A central cell is equilateral because its side relations are witnessed by equal-opening evidence.

4137. The visible appearance of the cell is not the source of its equality.

4138. The source of the equality is the preserved relation carried through construction.

4139. A figure whose claimed relations are source-earned can therefore support later proof.

4140. Therefore a constructed figure may now be treated as proof-bearing when its displayed relations are source-earned rather than visually assumed.

4141. The source record earns the central equilateral cell by equal-opening evidence.

4142. Repeated equal-opening construction places adjacent cells around the center.

4143. Six such central cells close the first complete turn.

4144. The hexagon can be read as an assembly of those six cells.

4145. This reading is not a visual decomposition imposed after the fact.

4146. It is a recomposition of source-earned cells.

4147. Later area, field, and catalogue claims may therefore refer to cell assembly when the relevant cell conditions are preserved.

4148. Therefore the completed source field may now be read through lawful cell assembly and recomposition.

4149. The source field produces stations through preserved constructional action.

4150. A station must exist before its position can be recorded.

4151. A coordinate or native record can remember how a station is reached.

4152. The record can compare reached stations after their constructional availability has been earned.

4153. The record does not create the station it records.

4154. The record does not create the relation it audits.

4155. Later coordinate use is lawful only when it remains an audit of prior constructional position.

4156. Therefore a coordinate or native record may audit constructed relations only after the construction has supplied the stations being recorded.

4157. The field contains stations produced by lawful constructional recurrence.

4158. Two earned stations may be compared by how one is reached from the other.

4159. A native difference records that comparison in the field's own recurring directions.

4160. The difference record depends on the prior availability of both stations.

4161. The difference record does not generate either station.

4162. The difference record can preserve the movement relation between the stations.

4163. Later span and direction audits may use the native difference when this dependency is respected.

4164. Therefore a relation between two field stations may now be recorded as a native difference when both stations have already been source-earned.

4165. A native difference records movement along two earned field directions.

4166. The two native field directions meet in the source field's sixty-degree structure.

4167. A straight span between the two recorded stations is not the same as a path count.

4168. The squared-span audit must account for both native direction components and their mutual relation.

4169. The native squared-span form records this audit as a^2 + ab + b^2.

4170. The form audits the span after the field has supplied the stations and directions.

4171. The form does not generate the span as a source object.

4172. Therefore a native difference may be audited by the squared-span form a^2 + ab + b^2 when the field basis and difference record have already been earned.

4173. A proposed span class must be realized by an earned relation in the source field.

4174. A native difference supplies the lawful record for testing such realization.

4175. The native squared norm audits which squared spans are available in the field.

4176. If no native difference realizes a proposed squared span, the span is not native to the field.

4177. A familiar outside length does not become available merely because it is known elsewhere.

4178. Square-grid expectation therefore cannot force a triangular-field span.

4179. Later exclusion claims may use the native norm as a filter when the audit conditions are explicit.

4180. Therefore the native squared norm may reject a proposed span class when no earned native difference realizes that class.

4181. A span class records the straight relation between two earned stations.

4182. Three span classes may each occur somewhere in the field.

4183. A triangle requires one triple of stations joined by three compatible spans.

4184. Individual occurrence does not supply that triple of stations.

4185. A side class may therefore be available without a corresponding triangle family being realized.

4186. Triangle realization is a stronger condition than span availability.

4187. Later right-triangle claims must prove triangle realization, not merely list three available lengths.

4188. Therefore the availability of individual span classes does not by itself realize a triangle with those classes as sides.

4189. The triangular field contains native differences between earned stations.

4190. A native difference may have a companion difference within the same field record.

4191. The companion relation can be audited for right-meeting by the field's own metric record.

4192. When the audit gives zero meeting product, the two differences are right-meeting in the audit sense.

4193. This rightness is not imported from a circle theorem.

4194. This rightness is not imported from a square grid.

4195. The field can therefore supply rightness through its own native companion structure.

4196. Therefore the triangular field may source-earn rightness through a native perpendicular-companion relation rather than through an imported diameter theorem.

4197. The native field contains a first unit span.

4198. The native perpendicular-companion structure gives a companion span whose squared length is three times the unit squared span.

4199. The companion side therefore has length root three.

4200. The closing side has squared length 1 + 3.

4201. The closing side therefore has length 2.

4202. The three sides form the field-right side pattern 1, root three, 2.

4203. This pattern is native to the triangular field before any trigonometric naming.

4204. Therefore the first native field-right triangle has side pattern 1, root three, 2.

4205. The side pattern 1, root three, 2 belongs to a source-earned field-right triangle.

4206. A right triangle distinguishes the side opposite the right meeting from the two meeting sides.

4207. The side opposite the right meeting is the hypotenuse.

4208. Choosing either acute angle distinguishes one leg as opposite and the other as adjacent.

4209. The side comparisons 1:2, root three:2, and 1:root three are then determined inside the triangle.

4210. These comparisons are ratio records before they are trigonometric names.

4211. Naming the ratios later compresses the already-earned comparison structure.

4212. Therefore the field-right triangle 1, root three, 2 makes a stable side-comparison packet active before sine, cosine, or tangent are named.

4213. The native span audit identifies root seven as a new oblique span beyond the first 1, root three, 2 packet.

4214. A new span class alone would only add a distance record.

4215. The field also realizes the side-squared family 3, 4, 7.

4216. Since 3 + 4 = 7, that family is right in the field-right audit.

4217. Its side lengths are root three, 2, root seven.

4218. The span root seven therefore becomes the hypotenuse of a realized right triangle.

4219. The new span has become a ratio-bearing structure rather than a merely isolated length.

4220. Therefore the first new oblique span root seven becomes structurally active when it appears as the hypotenuse of the field-right triangle root three, 2, root seven.

4221. A field can be expanded by different lawful rules.

4222. A radial expansion includes stations along selected rays.

4223. A full-shell expansion includes all stations within a shell condition.

4224. These two rules do not necessarily reveal the same relation at the same depth.

4225. A span or triangle family may appear earlier under one rule than another.

4226. A first-appearance statement is therefore incomplete without its expansion rule.

4227. Later first-appearance claims must state whether they are radial, full-shell, or otherwise rule-bound.

4228. Therefore a first-appearance claim is lawful only when the expansion rule under which it is made has been specified.

4229. The full theorem ledger asks what the construction can prove.

4230. A later mathematical domain does not require the whole ledger to be repeated.

4231. A domain requires a selected route through the earned permissions.

4232. That selected route is a mini ladder.

4233. A mini ladder is lawful only when each of its steps spends an earned affordance.

4234. The post-255 register identifies which affordances are active for such extraction.

4235. A fuse ladder may therefore test whether a new class of mathematical sentences can now land.

4236. Therefore after the source-ledger completion gate, a mini ladder may extract selected earned relations in order to test whether a mathematical domain has begun to speak.

4237. A fuse theorem is still a theorem.

6708. Therefore Equality-preserving transformations are permitted.

6709. An unknown is marked in an equation.

6710. The equation constrains that unknown.

6711. Equality-preserving transformations may change the equation's form.

6712. A changed form can move known records away from the unknown place.

6713. A changed form can expose the unknown relation more directly.

6714. The exposure remains lawful only when equality is preserved.

6715. Transformations may therefore isolate or expose the unknown relation.

6716. Therefore Transformations may isolate or expose the unknown relation.

6717. A recovered relation is proposed after transformation.

6718. The original equation supplied the constraint.

6719. The transformations preserved equality from the original equation.

6720. The recovered relation must fit the transformed form.

6721. The recovered relation must also fit the original constraint.

6722. A relation that fails the original constraint is not a solution.

6723. The recovered value or relation must therefore satisfy the original constraint.

6724. Therefore The recovered value or relation must satisfy the original constraint.

6725. Manipulation changes the form of a constrained equation.

6726. Equality-preserving manipulation keeps the original relation intact.

6727. The unknown was already constrained by the equation.

6728. The recovered relation comes from that constraint.

6729. The manipulation does not invent a new relation.

6730. A result without the original constraint would be arbitrary.

6731. The solution is therefore not invented by manipulation.

6732. Therefore The solution is not invented by manipulation.

6733. The equation constrains the unknown relation.

6734. Equality-preserving transformations expose that constraint.

6735. A recovered relation must satisfy the original equation.

6736. The satisfying relation was already fixed by the relation system.

6737. The solving process reveals that fixed relation.

6738. The process does not create an independent relation.

6739. The solution therefore recovers what the relation-system already constrained.

6740. Therefore The solution recovers what the relation-system already constrained.

6741. An unknown has been marked.

6742. An equation constrains the unknown.

6743. Equality-preserving transformations may expose the unknown relation.

6744. The recovered relation must satisfy the original constraint.

6745. The solution is not invented by manipulation.

6746. The solution recovers what the relation system already constrained.

6747. Solving is therefore relation recovery.

6748. Therefore solving is relation recovery.

6749. A relation is source-earned before it is recorded.

6750. The earning may come from construction, audit, equality evidence, or lawful recovery.

6751. The earned relation has a defined source boundary.

6752. The relation may be written only after that boundary is present.

6753. The written record must keep the source boundary traceable.

6754. A relation without source earning remains unavailable as proof.

6755. A relation has therefore been source-earned.

6756. Therefore A relation has been source-earned.

6757. A source-earned relation can be recorded in the ledger.

6758. A symbolic form may store that relation.

6759. The symbolic form points back to the source-earned record.

6760. The symbolic form does not replace the earning step.

6761. The symbolic form can be inspected later.

6762. Later inspection must preserve the defining conditions.

6763. The relation has therefore been recorded symbolically.

6764. Therefore The relation has been recorded symbolically.

6765. A relation record may contain known entries.

6766. A relation record may contain an unknown mark.

6767. Known entries keep their source evidence.

6768. Unknown entries keep their constrained status.

6769. An equation can hold both entries together.

6770. The record must not confuse known status with unknown status.

6771. The relation may therefore include knowns and unknowns.

6772. Therefore The relation may include knowns and unknowns.

6773. An equation records a source-grounded equality.

6774. Equality-preserving transformations may change its form.

6775. Each permitted transformation preserves the original constraint.

6776. A stable form may expose the relation for later use.

6777. The stable form remains tied to the original record.

6778. The stable form is reusable only under preserved conditions.

6779. Equality-preserving transformations have therefore produced a stable reusable form.

6780. Therefore Equality-preserving transformations have produced a stable reusable form.

6781. A stable symbolic form records a proven relation.

6782. The relation was earned before the form was written.

6783. The form preserves the relation's source boundary.

6784. The form can be cited later as memory.

6785. The citation must not replace the original proof.

6786. The citation must keep the defining conditions present.

6787. The symbolic form therefore remembers the proven relation.

6788. Therefore The symbolic form remembers the proven relation.

6789. A symbolic form remembers a proven relation.

6790. The relation has defining conditions.

6791. A later setting may preserve those defining conditions.

6792. When the conditions are preserved, the relation may be reused.

6793. When the conditions are not preserved, the form cannot travel.

6794. Reuse remains tied to the original proof memory.

6795. The form may therefore be reused elsewhere when defining conditions are preserved.

6796. Therefore The form may be reused elsewhere when defining conditions are preserved.

6797. A formula records a proven relation.

6798. The proof that earned the relation came before the formula.

6799. The formula keeps that proof available as memory.

6800. The formula does not create the relation by itself.

6801. The formula does not remove the source boundary.

6802. Using the formula without the proof conditions would be drift.

6803. The formula therefore does not replace the original proof.

6804. Therefore The formula does not replace the original proof.

6805. A formula stores a proven relation in compressed form.

6806. The stored relation remains tied to its proof source.

6807. A later diagram may preserve the defining conditions.

6808. The formula may then carry the proof memory into that diagram.

6809. The formula does not generate the later diagram.

6810. The formula remains lawful only when the preserved conditions are present.

6811. The formula therefore carries proof-memory beyond the original diagram.

6812. Therefore The formula carries proof-memory beyond the original diagram.

6813. A source-earned relation may be recorded symbolically.

6814. Equality-preserving transformations may produce a stable reusable form.

6815. The symbolic form remembers the proven relation.

6816. The form may be reused when defining conditions are preserved.

6817. The formula does not replace the original proof.

6818. The formula carries proof memory beyond the original diagram under those conditions.

6820. Therefore formula is portable proof memory.

6821. A constructional relation is earned by a source action.

6822. The relation has defining conditions.

6823. Those defining conditions may be preserved in later work.

6824. Preservation keeps the relation traceable to its source.

6825. The relation cannot travel without preservation.

6826. Later symbolic use must respect the preserved conditions.

6827. Constructional relations can therefore be preserved.

6828. Therefore Constructional relations can be preserved.

6834. Notation alone cannot justify the equality.

6835. Equal-opening evidence can therefore justify equality.

6837. A relation becomes available when source-earned.

6840. The record can later support symbolic memory.

6841. The record cannot exceed what the source earned.

6842. The record must remain distinguishable from unearned claims.

6843. Source-earned relations can therefore be recorded.

6844. Therefore Source-earned relations can be recorded.

6845. A source-earned relation may receive a symbol.

6846. The symbol preserves the relation for later inspection.

6847. The symbol points back to the source record.

6848. The symbol does not create new proof authority.

6849. Reuse requires preserved defining conditions.

6850. The symbol can travel only with those conditions.

6851. Symbols can therefore preserve relations for later reuse.

6852. Therefore Symbols can preserve relations for later reuse.

6853. A relation may be required before it is known.

6893. Source-earned relations can be recorded.

6894. Symbols can preserve those relations for later reuse.

6897. Equality-preserving transformations can expose constrained relations.
